4.
A finite presentation

Consider the even spin structure
\phi
on a surface
\Sigma_{g}^{1}
of genus
g
with one boundary component
C
defined by
\phi(C)=1
,
\phi(\alpha_{i})=1
and
\phi(\beta_{i})=0
for all
i=1,\dots,g
, in the notation of Figure
20
. In this section, we will find a finite presentation for
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g}^{1})[\phi]
and
\operatorname{Mod}(H_{g})[\phi]
, where
H_{g}
is the handlebody in which the
\alpha_{i}
bound disks.

Given group elements
a,b
, we will denote by
a*b
the conjugate
aba^{-1}
.

4.1.
The strategy

We start by recalling Hatcher and Thurston’s strategy (see also Laudenbach’s survey article
[
19
]
).

Fix a vertex
v_{0}\in X_{g}
. By the spin change of coordinates,
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g}^{1})[\phi]
acts transitively on the vertices of
X_{g}
, and we will see that there is a finite number of orbits of edges and faces with a vertex at
v_{0}
. For every orbit
O
of edges with a vertex at
v_{0}
, let
r_{O}\in\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g}^{1})[\phi]
be such that
v_{0}-r_{O}(v_{0})
is a representative of
O
. Call
S
the union of a generating set for
H[\phi]:=\operatorname{Stab}(v_{0})
and the elements
r_{O}
.

There is a correspondence between paths in
X_{g}
and words in
S
. Given
\varphi\in\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g}^{1})[\phi]
, by ?THM?
there is an edge-path
v_{0}-v_{1}-\dots-v_{k}=\varphi(v_{0})
. We can associate to such a path a word in
S
as follows. Let
O_{1}
be the edge orbit of
v_{0}-v_{1}
; then, there exists
h_{1}\in H[\phi]
such that
h_{1}^{-1}(v_{1})=r_{O_{1}}(v_{0})
, i.e.
h_{1}r_{O_{1}}(v_{0})=v_{1}
. Now, let
O_{2}
be the edge orbit of
v_{0}-(h_{1}r_{O_{1}})^{-1}(v_{2})
, and find
h_{2}\in H[\phi]
such that
h_{1}r_{O_{1}}h_{2}r_{O_{2}}(v_{0})=v_{2}
, and so on. Every
h_{i}
can be expressed as a word in the generators of
H[\phi]
, so the resulting
h
-product

h_{1}r_{O_{1}}\dots h_{k}r_{O_{k}}
is indeed a word in
S
. Moreover, we have
(h_{1}r_{1}\dots h_{k}r_{k})(v_{0})=\varphi(v_{0})
, so
\varphi^{-1}h_{1}r_{1}\dots h_{k}r_{k}
is equal to some
h_{k+1}^{-1}\in H[\phi]
and we can express
\varphi
as a word in
S
.

In the other direction, given an
h
-product
h_{1}r_{1}\dots h_{k}r_{k}
we can construct an edge path by setting
v_{i}:=h_{1}r_{1}\dots h_{i}r_{i}(v_{0})
for
i=0,1,\dots,k
. If an
h
-product
h_{1}r_{1}\dots h_{k}r_{k}
corresponds to a closed edge-path, then
h_{1}r_{1}\dots h_{k}r_{k}h_{k+1}
is a relation in
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g}^{1})[\phi]
, for some
h_{k+1}\in H[\phi]
.

We use this correspondence to prove the following theorem, which is the main result of this section.

Theorem 4.1
.

The group
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g}^{1})[\phi]
admits a finite presentation with generating set
S
and the following relations:

(A1)
-
(A8)

relations in the presentation of the stabilizer
H[\phi]
of
v_{0}
;

9

an
h
-product representing each path
v_{0}-r(v_{0})-v_{0}
, where
r\in S\setminus H[\phi]
;

10

all relations of the form
r^{-1}*h=h_{0}
, where
r\in S\setminus H[\phi]
,
h
is a generator of the stabilizer of the edge
v_{0}-r(v_{0})
and
h_{0}\in H[\phi]
;

11
-
14

an
h
-product representing each
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g}^{1})[\phi]
-orbit of faces in
X_{g}
with a vertex at
v_{0}
.

Proof.

Call
G
the group given by the presentation in the statement. Observe first that
H[\phi]
is finitely presented, as it is a finite index subgroup of the stabilizer
H
of
v_{0}
under the action of the full mapping class group, which is finitely presented by
[
31
, Proposition 27]
.

The above discussion shows that
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g}^{1})[\phi]
is a quotient of
G
. A relation in
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g}^{1})[\phi]
can be written as an
h
-product
h_{1}r_{1}\dots h_{k}r_{k}h_{k+1}
in
G
, which represents a closed edge path
\mathbf{p}
in
X_{g}
. We want to show that
h_{1}r_{1}\dots h_{k}r_{k}h_{k+1}
is equal to the identity in
G
.

First, any other
h
-product
h_{1}^{\prime}r_{1}^{\prime}\dots h_{k}^{\prime}r_{k}^{\prime}h_{k+1}^{\prime}
representing
\mathbf{p}
is equal to
h_{1}r_{1}\dots h_{k}r_{k}h_{k+1}
in
G
. Indeed, we have
r_{1}(v_{0})=h_{1}^{-1}h_{1}^{\prime}r_{1}^{\prime}(v_{0})
, hence
r_{1}
and
r_{1}^{\prime}
represent the same edge orbit and
r_{1}=r_{1}^{\prime}
. Moreover,
h_{1}^{-1}h_{1}^{\prime}
fixes the edge
v_{0}-r_{1}(v_{0})
, hence
10
gives
h_{1}^{-1}h_{1}^{\prime}r_{1}=r_{1}h_{1}^{\prime\prime}
for some
h_{1}^{\prime\prime}\in H[\phi]
. As a consequence,

h_{1}^{\prime}r_{1}^{\prime}h_{2}^{\prime}r_{2}^{\prime}\dots h_{k}^{\prime}r_{k}^{\prime}h_{k+1}^{\prime}=h_{1}r_{1}h_{1}^{\prime\prime}h_{2}^{\prime}r_{2}^{\prime}\dots h_{k}^{\prime}r_{k}^{\prime}h_{k+1}^{\prime},

so we get two shorter
h
-products representing the same edge-path and we conclude by induction on
k
.

Moreover, we can assume that
\mathbf{p}
does not contain
backtrackings
, i.e. subpaths
v_{i}-v_{i+1}-v_{i+2}
where
v_{i}=v_{i+2}
. Indeed, if there is such a subpath, we may assume that it is represented by a conjugate of an
h
-product representing the path
v_{0}-r(v_{0})-v_{0}
for some generator
r
, and these are trivial in
G
by
9
.

Finally, by ?THM?

\mathbf{p}
is null-homotopic, hence it can be written as a composition of paths that go from
v_{0}
to some vertex
v
, then go around a face of
X_{g}
and finally go back from
v
to
v_{0}
along the same path as before. By the above discussion, we can assume that these paths are represented by conjugates of
h
-product representing faces with a vertex at
v_{0}
, which are trivial in
G
by
11
-
14
.

As the number of orbits of edges and faces of
X_{g}
touching
v_{0}
is finite, the resulting presentation is finite. ∎

.

4.2.
Stabilizer of a vertex

Consider the spin cut-system
v_{0}=\Braket{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{g}}
of Figure
20
. We are going to give a presentation of its stabilizer
H[\phi]
under the action of
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g}^{1})[\phi]
, which is a finite-index subgroup of the stabilizer
H
of
v_{0}
under the action of
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g}^{1})
. We will apply the Nielsen-Schreier method to Wajnryb’s presentation of
H

[
31
, Proposition 27]
.

First of all, we introduce Wajnryb’s generators for
H
. In the notation of Figure
20
, set
a_{i}:=t_{\alpha_{i}}
for
i=1,\dots,g
,
s:=t_{\beta_{1}}t_{\alpha_{1}}^{2}t_{\beta_{1}}
and
t_{i}:=t_{\varepsilon_{i}}t_{\alpha_{i}}t_{\alpha_{i+1}}t_{\varepsilon_{i}}
for

. Moreover, for all

with

, let

be the curve in Figure

, and set
d_{i,j}:=t_{\delta_{i,j}}
and

(6)

\overline{d}_{i,j}:=d_{i,j}a_{\lvert i\rvert}^{-1}a_{\lvert j\rvert}^{-1}.

Proposition 4.2
.

The group
H[\phi]
admits a presentation with generators
a_{1}^{2},\dots,a_{g}^{2}
,
s
,
t_{1},\dots
,
t_{g-1}
and
\overline{d}_{i,j}
for all
i,j\in\set{\pm 1,\dots,\pm g}
with
i<j
, and the following relations:

(A1)

[a_{i}^{2},a_{j}^{2}]=1
and
[a_{i}^{2},\overline{d}_{j,k}]=1
for all
i,j,k
;

(A2)

pure braid relations:

(a)

\overline{d}_{r,s}^{-1}*\overline{d}_{i,j}=\overline{d}_{i,j}
if
r<s<i<j
or
i<r<s<j
;

(b)

\overline{d}_{r,s}^{-1}*\overline{d}_{s,j}=\overline{d}_{r,j}*\overline{d}_{s,j}
if
r<s<j
;

(c)

\overline{d}_{r,j}^{-1}*\overline{d}_{r,s}=\overline{d}_{s,j}*\overline{d}_{r,s}
if
r<s<j
;

(d)

[\overline{d}_{i,j},\overline{d}_{r,j}^{-1}*\overline{d}_{r,s}]=1
if
r<i<s<j
;

(A3)

t_{i}t_{i+1}t_{i}=t_{i+1}t_{i}t_{i+1}
for all
i
and
[t_{i},t_{j}]=1
if
i<j-1
;

(A4)

s^{2}=\overline{d}_{-1,1}a_{1}^{-2}
and
t_{i}^{2}=\overline{d}_{i,i+1}\overline{d}_{-i-1,-i}
for all
i
;

(A5)

[t_{i},s]=1
for all
i\geq 2
;

(A6)

st_{1}st_{1}=t_{1}st_{1}s
;

(A7)

[s,a_{i}^{2}]=1
for all
i
,
t_{i}*a_{i}^{2}=a_{i+1}^{2}
for all
i
and
[a_{i}^{2},t_{j}]=1
if
j\neq i,i-1
;

(A8)

other relations involving the generators
\overline{d}_{i,j}
:

(a)

s*\overline{d}_{i,j}=\overline{d}_{i,j}
if
\lvert i\rvert,\lvert j\rvert\geq 2
or if
i=-1
and
j=1
,
s*\overline{d}_{-1,j}=\overline{d}_{1,j}
if
j\geq 2
,
s*\overline{d}_{i,-1}=\overline{d}_{i,1}
if
i\leq-2
;

(b)

t_{k}*\overline{d}_{i,j}=\overline{d}_{i,j}
if
j-1=i=k
or
j=i+1=-k
or
\lvert i\rvert,\lvert j\rvert\neq k,k+1
;

(c)

t_{k}*\overline{d}_{k,j}=\overline{d}_{k+1,j}
if
j\geq k+2
and
t_{k}*\overline{d}_{i,-k-1}=\overline{d}_{i,-k}
if
i\leq-k-2
;

(d)

t_{k}*\overline{d}_{-k-1,k}=\overline{d}_{-k,k+1}
;

(e)

t_{k}*\overline{d}_{-k-1,k+1}=\overline{d}_{k,k+1}*\overline{d}_{-k,k}
;

(f)

t_{k}*\overline{d}_{-k-1,j}=\overline{d}_{-k,j}
if
j>-k
and
j\neq k,k+1
and
t_{k}*\overline{d}_{i,k}=\overline{d}_{i,k+1}
if
i<k
and
i\neq-k,-k-1
.

Notice that relations (A4) and (A8) allow us to eliminate all the generators
\overline{d}_{i,j}
apart from one, for example
\overline{d}_{1,2}
.

Proof.

First of all, we apply the following Tietze moves to the presentation of
H
given by
[
31
, Proposition 27]
. We add generators
\overline{d}_{i,j}
for all
i,j
and relations (
6
). The
d_{i,j}
only appear in relations (P1), (P2), (P4) and (P8), and can be replaced by the
\overline{d}_{i,j}
using (
6
).

(P1)

Since all the generators
a_{i}
commute, we obtain
[\alpha_{i},\overline{d}_{j,k}]=1
.

(P2)

The pure braid relations only involve the generators
d_{i,j}
; again, since all
a_{i}
commute, we just replace
d_{i,j}
with
\overline{d}_{i,j}
for all
i,j
, obtaining (A2).

(P4)

The relations become
s^{2}=\overline{d}_{-1,1}a_{1}^{-2}
and
t_{i}^{2}=\overline{d}_{i,i+1}\overline{d}_{-i-1,-i}
for all
i
.

(P8)

By relations (P7),
s
commutes with all the
a_{i}
, while
t_{i}*a_{i}=a_{i+1}
and
[a_{i},t_{j}]=1
if
j\neq i,i-1
. Notice that by (P1) and (P4) we also have

(7)

t_{i}*a_{i+1}=t_{i}^{2}*a_{i}=(\overline{d}_{i,i+1}\overline{d}_{-i-1,-i})*a_{i}=a_{i}.

Therefore, we just have to replace each
d_{i,j}
with
\overline{d}_{i,j}
, obtaining (A8).

Now, we can remove generators
d_{i,j}
and relations
6
from the presentation of
H
. Notice that all the new generators of
H
preserve
\phi
, apart from
a_{1},\dots,a_{g}
.

We claim that the subgroup
H[\phi]
of
H
is generated by the elements
a_{i}^{2}
,
s
,
t_{i}
and
\overline{d}_{i,j}
. Indeed, let
w
be a word in the generators of
H
. By relations (P1), (P7) and (
7
), we can write it as
w=w^{\prime}a_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}}\dots a_{g}^{\epsilon_{g}}
, where
w^{\prime}
is a word in the generators
s
,
t_{i}
and
\overline{d}_{i,j}
. Therefore,
w^{\prime}
represents an element of
H[\phi]
, and by ?THM?
(2)
w
represents an element of
H[\phi]
if and only if each
\varepsilon_{i}
is even. As a consequence, a Schreier transversal for
H^{s}
in
H
is

(8)

U:=\bigg\{u_{J}:=\prod_{j\in J}a_{j}\bigg|J\subseteq\set{1,\dots,g}\bigg\},

ordered lexicographically.

Now we determine the Schreier generators for
H^{s}
. Recall that they are of the form
ux\overline{ux}^{-1}
, where
u
is an element of
U
,
x
or
x^{-1}
is a generator of
H
, and
g\mapsto\overline{g}
is the function
H\rightarrow U
that sends every element to the unique representative in
U
of its
H[\phi]
-coset.

Observe that if
x
or
x^{-1}
is equal to
s
or to
\overline{d}_{i,j}
for some
i,j
, then it commutes with all the elements of
U
; hence, in this case, we have
\overline{ux}=u
for every
u\in U
. If
x=a_{i}^{\pm 1}
, we have

\overline{u_{J}a_{i}^{\pm 1}}=\begin{cases}u_{J\cup\set{i}}&\text{if}\ i\notin J,\\
u_{J\setminus\set{i}}&\text{if}\ i\in J.\end{cases}

If
x=t_{i}^{\pm 1}
we have:

\overline{u_{J}t_{i}^{\pm 1}}=\begin{cases}u_{J}&\text{if}\ i,i+1\in J\ \ \text{or}\ i,i+1\notin J;\\
u_{(J\setminus\set{i})\cup\set{i+1}}&\text{if}\ i\in J\ \ \text{and}\ i+1\notin J;\\
u_{(J\setminus\set{i+1})\cup\set{i}}&\text{if}\ i\notin J\ \ \text{and}\ i+1\in J.\end{cases}

In order to streamline the process, we can use directly the relations of
H
to get rid of redundant generators. For example, by (P7)
s
commutes with all the elements of
U
in
H
, hence all the generators
us\overline{us}^{-1}
coincide with
s
in
H[\phi]
. More generally, using relations (P1) and (P7), we see that the Schreier generators boil down exactly to those in the statement. Indeed, each generator
g
of
H
that preserves
\phi
gives a family of Schreier generators which are all equal to
g
itself or to a product of
g
and some
a_{i}^{2}
, and for all
i
we have

u_{J}a_{i}\overline{u_{J}a_{i}}^{-1}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if}\ i\in J,\\
a_{i}^{2}&\text{if}\ i\notin J.\end{cases}

Finally, the relations for
H[\phi]
are of the form
uru^{-1}
, where
u
is an element of
U
and
r
is a relation for
H
. Clearly, the only relations that change are the ones involving some
a_{i}
, that is, (P1), (P4) and (P7).

(P1)

We obtain
[\alpha_{i}^{2},\alpha_{j}^{2}]=1
and
[\alpha_{i}^{2},\overline{d}_{j,k}]=1
for all
i,j,k
.

(P4)

The first relation becomes
s^{2}=\overline{d}_{-1,1}(a_{1}^{2})^{-1}
.

(P7)

We get
[s,a_{i}^{2}]=1
for all
i
,
t_{i}*a_{i}^{2}=a_{i+1}^{2}
for all
i
and
[a_{i}^{2},t_{j}]=1
if
j\neq i,i-1
. ∎

4.3.
Orbits of edges

We can now derive a complete set of generators for
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g}^{1})[\phi]
. Consider the action of
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g}^{1})[\phi]
on the edges of
X_{g}
starting at
v_{0}
. Clearly, the orbits of edges of type (i) are disjoint from those of edges of type (ii). By the spin change of coordinates, that there is a unique orbit of edges of type (i). A representative for this orbit is the edge
v_{0}-b_{1}(v_{0})
, where
b_{1}=\tau_{\beta_{1}}
in the notations of Figure
20
.

For the edges of type (ii), the situation is akin to the one considered by Wajnryb in his paper on the handlebody group
[
32
]
. Wajnryb studies the action of
\operatorname{Mod}(H_{g})
on a cell complex
X_{g}^{H}
whose vertices are cut-systems of meridians for
H_{g}
, and where two vertices
\Braket{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{g}}
and
\Braket{\alpha_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\alpha_{g}^{\prime}}
are connected by an edge if
\lvert\alpha_{1}\cap\alpha_{1}^{\prime}\rvert=0
and
\alpha_{k}=\alpha_{k}^{\prime}
for
k=2,\dots,g
.

Now, consider an edge of type (ii)
v_{0}-v_{1}
in
X_{g}
. Up to renaming the curves,
v_{1}
is of the form
\Braket{\gamma_{1},\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{g}}
, where the curve
\gamma_{1}
intersects
\alpha_{1}
twice (algebraically and geometrically) and is disjoint from
\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{g}
. Cutting
\Sigma_{g}^{1}
along
\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{g}
, we get a torus
T
with a number of boundary components, that inherits an even spin structure. Capping each boundary component with a disk, we get a closed torus
\overline{T}
, and we can complete
\alpha_{1}
to a geometric symplectic basis
\set{\alpha_{1},\eta_{1}}
. Note that there are just two possible choices for
\eta_{1}
up to squared Dehn twists along
\alpha_{1}
. Now,
\gamma_{1}
corresponds to a curve
\overline{\gamma_{1}}
on
\overline{T}
whose homology class is
(2k+1)\alpha_{1}\pm 2\eta_{1}
. Again, up to squared twists along
\alpha_{1}
, we may suppose that
k
is either
0
or
-1
. Hence, we get exactly two possible isotopy classes for
\overline{\gamma_{1}}
up to the action of
\operatorname{Mod}(\overline{T})[\phi]
.

If
H_{g}
is the handlebody with meridians
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{g}
, the above argument shows that
\gamma_{1}
is given by
\tau_{\eta_{1}}^{\pm 2}(\xi_{1})
, where
\eta_{1}
is a curve that intersects
\alpha_{1}
once and is disjoint from
\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{g}
, and there is an edge

\Braket{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{g}}-\Braket{\xi_{1},\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{g}}

in Wajnryb’s complex
X_{g}^{H}
. Up to the action of
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g}^{1})[\phi]
, we may suppose that
\eta_{1}=\beta_{1}
(note that we did not require
\eta_{1}
to be spin). Wajnryb classifies the possible choices for
\xi_{1}
up to the action of
\operatorname{Mod}(H_{g})
(see
[
32
, page 220]
), but the classification up to the action of
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g}^{1})[\phi]
is exactly the same: a mapping class that fixes
\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{g}
and sends
\xi_{1}
to another possible choice
\xi_{1}^{\prime}
necessarily extends to
H_{g}
, and can be made spin by composing it with suitable twists along
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{g}
.

Remark 4.3
.

Cutting
\Sigma_{g}^{1}
along all the curves involved in an edge of type (ii) yields a planar surface with two connected components, one of which contains the hole coming from the boundary component
C
of
\Sigma_{g}^{1}
. Hence, we actually have more edge orbits, according to the component on which
C
sits. We will see that the faces of
X_{g}
containing these extra orbits are superfluous for the simple connectivity, as a consequence of ?THM?
and ?THM?
. Hence, we are going to ignore them.

We can now give a system of representatives for the orbits of edges of type (ii). The system of representatives found by Wajnryb is given by the edges
\Braket{\alpha_{j}}-\Braket{\gamma_{i,j}}
for
i,j\in\set{\pm 1,\dots,\pm g}
,
i\leq 1
,
j+i\geq 1
,
j-i\leq g
, where
\gamma_{i,j}
is the curve in Figure
21
. Define mapping classes
r_{i,j}=b_{j}a_{j}c_{i,j}b_{j}
, where
c_{i,j}:=t_{\gamma_{i,j}}
. Note that
r_{i,j}
swaps
\alpha_{j}
and
\gamma_{i,j}
, and fixes all other curves
\alpha_{k}
. Moreover, in homology mod
2
we have

\big[r_{i,j}(\beta_{k})\big]\equiv_{2}\begin{cases}[\beta_{k}]&\text{if}\ k\leq\lvert i\rvert\ \text{and}\ i\leq-1,\ \ \text{or if}\ k\geq j+1,\\
[\beta_{k}+\gamma_{i,j}+\beta_{j}]&\text{if}\ k=i=1,\ \ \text{or if}\ \lvert i\rvert<k<j,\\
[\alpha_{j}+\beta_{j}+\gamma_{i,j}]&\text{if}\ k=j.\end{cases}

In particular,
r_{i,j}
only changes the spin value in the second case. Set

(9)

\overline{r}_{i,j}:=\begin{cases}a_{1}^{-1}\dots a_{j-1}^{-1}r_{1,j}&if\text{i=1},\\
a_{1}^{-2}\dots a_{-i}^{-2}a_{-i+1}^{-1}\dots a_{j-1}^{-1}r_{i,j}&\text{if $i\leq-1$}.\end{cases}

Clearly,
\overline{r}_{i,j}
preserves the spin structure, swaps
\alpha_{j}
and
\gamma_{i,j}
, and fixes all the other curves
\alpha_{k}
. Thus, a system of representatives for the orbits of edges of type (ii) under the action of
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g}^{1})[\phi]
is given by
v_{0}-v^{\pm}
and
v_{0}-v_{i,j}^{\pm}
, where

v^{\pm}:=b_{1}^{\pm 2}(v_{0}),\quad v_{i,j}^{\pm}:=b_{j}^{\pm 2}\overline{r}_{i,j}(v_{0}).

Here, the indices
i,j
are elements of
\set{\pm 1,\dots,\pm g}
such that
i\leq 1
,
j+i\geq 1
and
j-i\leq g
.

Our generating set
S
for
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g}^{1})[\phi]
is then given by the generators of
H[\phi]
from ?THM?
,
b
,
b_{1}^{\pm 2}
and
b_{j}^{\pm 2}\overline{r}_{i,j}
for
i,j
as above.

Remark 4.4
.

We only used the connectivity of the spin cut-system complex to determine a generating set for
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g}^{1})[\phi]
. By ?THM?
, edges of type (ii) are not necessary for the connectivity, hence generators
b_{1}^{\pm 2}
and
b_{j}^{\pm}\overline{r}_{i,j}
are superfluous. We will keep them for now as they are needed to write the relations. Notice that for now we should also treat
b_{1}
,
b_{1}^{2}
and
b_{1}^{-2}
as independent generators. From
9
and
11
we will obtain the obvious relations between them.

4.4.
Backtracking

We now write explicitly relations
9
. Recall that these are
h
-products representing
backtrackings
, i.e. loops of the form
v_{0}-r(v_{0})-v_{0}
, for every generator
r\notin H[\phi]
.

The curve

We first introduce some additional notations. Cut
\Sigma_{g}^{1}
along the curves
\alpha_{0},\dots,\alpha_{g}
, obtaining a planar surface as in Figure
21
. Given a subset
I
of
\set{\pm 1,\dots,\pm g}
, let
\delta_{I}
be the curve that encircles the red holes corresponding to the elements of
I
, where the upper holes are indexed by negative integers (see Figure
23
). In particular,
\delta_{i,j}=\delta_{\set{i,j}}
, and
\gamma_{i,j}=\delta_{\set{i,i+1,\dots,j}}
. Finally, set
d_{I}:=t_{\delta_{I}}
. We define

(10)

\overline{d}_{\set{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}}:=d_{\set{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}}(a_{i_{1}}\dots a_{i_{n}})^{-1}.

It can be shown that

(11)

\overline{d}_{\set{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}}=(\overline{d}_{i_{1},i_{2}}\overline{d}_{i_{1},i_{3}}\dots\overline{d}_{i_{1},i_{n}}\overline{d}_{i_{2},i_{3}}\dots\overline{d}_{i_{2},i_{n}}\dots\overline{d}_{i_{n-1},i_{n}}).

Remark 4.5
.

The expansion of (
11
) involves a number of lantern relations. The idea is the following. Consider the lantern specified by the curves
\delta_{i_{n-1},i_{n}}
and
\delta_{I\setminus\set{i_{n}}}
, i.e.

d_{i_{n-1},i_{n}}d_{I\setminus\set{i_{n}}}d_{I\setminus\set{i_{n-1}}}=a_{\lvert i_{n-1}\rvert}a_{\lvert i_{n}\rvert}d_{I}d_{I\setminus\set{i_{n-1},i_{n}}}.

This allows us to write
d_{I}
in terms of mapping classes
d_{I^{\prime}}
, where
I^{\prime}
has one or two elements less than
I
(see Figure
22
). Notice that if we denote by
\ell(n)
the number of lanterns needed to write
d_{I}
as a product of elements
d_{i,j}
, we can write a recurrence relation and see that
\ell(n)=(n-1)(n-2)/2
.

Figure 23.
The lantern
xyz=a_{3}a_{4}d_{-3,-1}d_{I}
can be used to define

for

. Here
x=d_{3,4}
,

and

.

Moreover, set
k_{j}:=t_{j}\overline{d}_{j,j+1}^{-1}
for all

, and define
s_{1}:=s
and

It can be shown that

(see (

)).

Remark 4.6
.

Backtracking on an edge in the orbit of
v_{0}-v_{i,j}^{+}
results in an edge in the orbit of
v_{0}-v_{i,j}^{-}
. This can be seen by assigning orientations to the curves intersecting twice. Hence, it suffices to consider the
h
-products relative to backtrackings where the first edge is
v_{0}-v_{i,j}^{+}
.

(9)

We have
b_{1}a_{1}^{2}b_{1}=s_{1}
,
b_{1}^{+2}b_{1}^{-2}=1
,

b_{j}^{+2}\overline{r}_{1,j}\overline{d}_{\set{1,\dots,j}}a_{j}^{2}b_{j}^{-2}\overline{r}_{1,j}=a_{1}^{-2}\dots a_{j-1}^{-2}a_{j}^{2}s_{j}\overline{d}_{\set{1,\dots,j}}s_{j}

for every

j\geq 2

and

b_{j}^{+2}\overline{r}_{i,j}\overline{d}_{\set{i,\dots,j}}a_{j}^{2}b_{j}^{-2}\overline{r}_{i,j}=a_{1}^{-4}\dots a_{-i}^{-4}a_{-i+1}^{-2}\dots a_{j-1}^{-2}a_{j}^{2}s_{j}\overline{d}_{\set{i,\dots,j}}s_{j}

for every

i,j\in\set{\pm 1,\dots,\pm g}

with
i\leq-1
,
j+i\geq 1
and
j-i\leq g
.

Proof of
9
.

The first two relations are clear. The other relations follow from braid relations
T_{1}
(in the whole
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g}^{1})
). We do the case
i=1
; the other is similar. We underline the places where a relation
T_{1}
is applied:

\displaystyle b_{j}^{\pm 2}\overline{r}_{1,j}\overline{d}_{\set{1,\dots,j}}a_{j}^{2}b_{j}^{\mp 2}\overline{r}_{1,j}=(a_{1}\dots a_{j-1})^{-3}b_{j}^{\pm 2}r_{1,j}c_{1,j}a_{j}b_{j}^{\mp 2}r_{i,j}=

\displaystyle\quad=(a_{1}\dots a_{j-1})^{-3}b_{j}b_{j}^{\pm 2}a_{j}\underline{c_{1,j}b_{j}c_{1,j}}a_{j}b_{j}^{\mp 2}b_{j}a_{j}c_{1,j}b_{j}=

\displaystyle\quad=(a_{1}\dots a_{j-1})^{-3}b_{j}\underline{b_{j}^{\pm 2}a_{j}b_{j}}c_{1,j}\underline{b_{j}a_{j}b_{j}^{\mp 2}}b_{j}a_{j}c_{1,j}b_{j}=

\displaystyle\quad=(a_{1}\dots a_{j-1})^{-3}\underline{b_{j}a_{j}b_{j}}a_{j}^{\pm 2}c_{1,j}a_{j}^{\mp 2}\underline{b_{j}a_{j}b_{j}}a_{j}c_{1,j}b_{j}=

\displaystyle\quad=(a_{1}\dots a_{j-1})^{-3}a_{j}b_{j}a_{j}a_{j}\underline{c_{1,j}b_{j}c_{1,j}}a_{j}a_{j}b_{j}=a_{1}^{-2}\dots a_{j-1}^{-2}a_{j}^{2}s_{j}\overline{d}_{\set{1,\dots,j}}s_{j}.\qed

With the relations of ?THM?
and
9
, we can already obtain the following small set of generators, which may be of interest.

Corollary 4.7
.

The even spin mapping class group
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g})[\phi]
is generated by
a_{1}^{2},b_{1},t_{1},\overline{d}_{1,2}
and
u:=t_{1}\dots t_{g-1}
.

Proof.

Call
G
the subgroup of
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g})[\phi]
generated by the elements in the statement. By
9
,
s_{1}=b_{1}a_{1}^{2}b_{1}
is contained in
G
. Thus, by
(A3)
,
(A4)
,
(A7)
and
(A8)
, all the
a_{i}^{2}
, the
\overline{d}_{i,j}
and the
t_{i}
are also contained in
G
. As noted in ?THM?
, the generators
b_{1}^{\pm 2}
and
b_{j}^{\pm 2}\overline{r}_{i,j}
are superfluous, so
G=\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g})[\phi]
. ∎

4.5.

-product to the same edge. In order to write down explicitly such relations, we must find a generating set for the stabilizer of each class of edges.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=c_{i,j-1}\big((t_{\lvert i\rvert+1}\dots t_{j-1})*c_{i-1,j}\big).\qed

4.6.
Faces

The last set of relations comes from the 2-cells in our complex. We are going to establish a list of closed edge paths
\mathbf{p}_{i}
, such that every closed edge path is a sum of paths conjugate to some
\mathbf{p}_{i}
, i.e. of the form
\mathbf{q}_{1}\mathbf{q}_{2}\mathbf{q}_{1}^{-1}
, where
\mathbf{q}_{1}
starts at
v_{0}
and
\mathbf{q}_{2}
is the image of some
\mathbf{p}_{i}
under the action of
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g}^{1})[\phi]
. The relations will be the
h
-products associated to the paths
\mathbf{p}_{i}
. For the proof that these relations hold in
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g}^{1})[\phi]
, see Subsection
5.3
.

Triangles.
We apply Harer’s reduction process
[
12
]
. We explain this method in detail for triangles involving an edge of type
(i,j)^{+}
; the negative case is symmetric. Let
\mathbf{p}
be a triangle
v_{0}-v_{1}-v_{2}-v_{0}
. Cut
\Sigma_{g}^{1}
along the

curves in common, obtaining a

-holed torus

with three curves
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
, where

,

and

, and the edge of type (ii) is
\Braket{\alpha}-\Braket{\gamma}
. Call

the closed torus obtained by capping all boundary components with disks. Then the universal cover of
\widehat{T}
has a fundamental region which is a square with edges along
\alpha
and

. Orient the three curves in such a way that
(\alpha,\beta)=1
,

and

(a). Note that changing the orientations of all three curves switches the roles of
F_{0}
and

and of

and

.

Lemma 4.10
.

Every triangle is a sum of paths conjugated to triangles with
\ell_{0}=0
and
\ell_{3}\leq 1
, where the hole corresponding to the boundary component of
\Sigma_{g}^{1}
lies in

Proof.

First of all, we prove that every triangle is a sum of paths conjugated to triangles with
\ell_{3}\leq 1
. Indeed, if a triangle
\Braket{\alpha}-\Braket{\gamma}-\Braket{\beta}-\Braket{\alpha}
has

, we consider push-offs
\alpha^{\prime}
,
\beta^{\prime}
and

as in Figure

, apart from the original one, and we can iterate the process until every triangle has up to 1 hole in region
F_{3}
. Note that
\ell_{0}
stays the same throughout, so we can switch
F_{0}
and
F_{3}
and repeat the process until every triangle has up to 1 hole in both regions.

We only have to deal with the case

. Consider push-offs
\alpha^{\prime}
,
\beta^{\prime}
and
\gamma^{\prime}
as in Figure

F_{0}
or
F_{3}
without holes, and if one of them contains two holes we can do the same process as before.

Up to changing the orientations of the curves, we are done. Notice that if the hole corresponding to the boundary components of
\Sigma_{g}^{1}
lies in

(or
F_{2}
), we can slide

on the whole
F_{0}
, so that in the new configuration the hole has moved to

(
F_{1}
), and then repeat the above process, which does not remove any hole from
F_{2}
(
F_{1}
). ∎

Lemma 4.11
.

All pentagons are homotopic in

.

Proof.

Consider another pentagon in

-chain on

we may assume that it is of the form

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(ii)}

for some curve

We get the following relation:

b_{2}^{-2}\overline{r}_{1,2}\overline{d}_{-2,-1}\overline{d}_{1,2}^{-1}b_{1}t_{1}a_{1}^{2}b_{1}\overline{d}_{-2,-1}^{-3}t_{1}b_{1}t_{1}b_{1}s_{2}=\overline{d}_{\set{-2,-1,1,2}}

Hyperelliptic faces.
Recall that a hyperelliptic face is uniquely determined by a
7
-chain of admissible curves by ?THM?
. Moreover, from the proof of ?THM?
and ?THM?
we see that we need only the hyperelliptic faces corresponding to

-chains that split the surface into two components, one of which has genus
0
and does not intersect
\partial\Sigma_{g}^{1}
. By the spin change of coordinates principle, we just need a single relation.

Instead of writing a long
h
-product, we can directly state the relation as a product of admissible twist as follows. Fix a
7
-chain of admissible curves
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{7}\subset\Sigma_{g}^{1}
with the above properties, and let
\delta
be the nontrivial boundary component of a tubular neighborhood of
\gamma_{1}\cup\dots\cup\gamma_{7}
. Then we have the following restatement of (
3
):

(14)

(t_{\gamma_{1}}\dots t_{\gamma_{6}}t_{\gamma_{7}}^{2}t_{\gamma_{6}}\dots t_{\gamma_{1}})^{2}=t_{\delta}
.

4.7.
A finite presentation of the spin handlebody group

We conclude this section with a finite presentation for the spin handlebody mapping class group
\operatorname{Mod}(H_{g})[\phi]
, where
H_{g}
is the handlebody in which the curves
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{g}
of Figure
20
bound disks. This will be done using Nielsen-Schreier’s method, and will not be needed in the following, but is relevant for
[
3
]
.

Theorem 4.12
.

The spin handlebody mapping class group
\operatorname{Mod}(H_{g})[\phi]
has a presentation with generators
a_{1}^{2},\dots,a_{g}^{2}
,
s
,
t_{1},\dots,t_{g-1}
,
\overline{d}_{i,j}
for all
i,j\in\set{\pm 1,\dots,\pm g}
with
i<j
and
\overline{r}_{i,j}
for all
i,j\in\set{\pm 1,\dots,\pm g}
with
i=1<j
or
i\leq-1
and
-i+1\leq j\leq g+i
, and the following relations:

(H7)

triangle relations:

\displaystyle\overline{r}_{1,j}

\displaystyle=s_{j}\overline{d}_{\set{1,\dots,j}}s_{j}\overline{d}_{\set{1,\dots,j}}^{-1}k_{j-1}\overline{d}_{\set{1,\dots,j-2}}t_{j-1}\overline{d}_{\set{1,\dots,j-1}}^{-1}t_{j-1}^{-1}\cdot

\displaystyle\overline{r}_{-1,j}

\displaystyle=Ba_{1}^{-2}\overline{r}_{1,2}^{-1}B^{-1}s_{j}\overline{r}_{1,j}^{-1}(a_{1}a_{2}\dots a_{j-1})^{-2}\overline{d}_{\set{-1,\dots,j-1}}^{-1}\cdot

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\cdot\overline{d}_{\set{1,\dots,j-1}}s_{j}\overline{d}_{\set{-1,\dots,j}}s_{j}\overline{d}_{\set{-1,\dots,j}}^{-1},

\displaystyle\overline{r}_{i,j}

\displaystyle=Ca_{1}^{-1}\overline{r}_{1,2}^{-1}C^{-1}s_{j}(a_{1}^{2}\dots a_{-i-1}^{2}a_{-i}\dots a_{j-1})^{-2}\overline{r}_{i+1,j}^{-1}\overline{d}_{\set{i,\dots,j-1}}^{-1}\cdot

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\cdot\overline{d}_{\set{i+1,\dots,j-1}}s_{j}\overline{d}_{\set{i,\dots,j}}s_{j}\overline{d}_{\set{i,\dots,j}}^{-1}\quad\text{for $i\leq-2$},

where

\displaystyle A=k_{j-1}^{-1}t_{j-2}^{-1}t_{j-3}^{-1}\dots t_{1}^{-1}k_{j-1}k_{j-2}\dots k_{2},

\displaystyle B=s_{1}k_{j-1}k_{j-2}\dots k_{2},

\displaystyle C=s_{-i}t_{-i-1}^{-1}t_{-i-2}^{-1}\dots t_{1}^{-1}k_{j-1}k_{j-2}\dots k_{2}.

Proof.

We will apply the Nielsen-Schreier method to Wajnryb’s presentation of
\operatorname{Mod}(H_{g})

[
32
, Theorem 18]
. First of all, by inspecting his proof, it is easy to see that an equivalent presentation is given by the presentation
[
31
, Proposition 27]
of the stabilizer of
v_{0}=\Braket{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{g}}
in
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g}^{1})
, together with generators
r_{i,j}:=b_{j}a_{j}c_{i,j}b_{j}
and relations (P3), (P4), (P9), (P10), (P11) and (P12) of
[
32
, Theorem 18]
. Indeed, since
v_{0}
is a cut-system of meridians for
H_{g}
, its stabilizers under the action of
\operatorname{Mod}(H_{g})
and
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g})
coincide, and relations (P3), (P4) of
[
32
, Theorem 18]
come from capping the boundary component of
\Sigma_{g}^{1}
with a disk. Relation (P8) of
[
32
, Theorem 18]
is clearly implied by relation (P8) of
[
31
, Proposition 27]
, which on the other hand still holds in
\operatorname{Mod}(H_{g})
. The last four relations of
[
32
, Theorem 18]
are derived by studying the action of
\operatorname{Mod}(H_{g})
on a complex of cut-system of meridians.

Now, we proceed exactly as in the proof of ?THM?
. We introduce new generators
\overline{d}_{i,j}
and
\overline{r}_{i,j}
, and new relations (
6
) and (

). The relations coming from
[
31
, Proposition 27]
change as in the proof of ?THM?
, while the other relations change as follows.

(P3)

From (
10
) we get
\overline{d}_{\set{\pm 1,\dots,\pm g}}(a_{1}\dots a_{g})^{2}=1
.

(P4)

Similarly, we obtain
\overline{d}_{\set{\pm 1,\dots,\pm g}\setminus\set{k}}(a_{1}\dots\widehat{a_{\lvert k\rvert}}\dots a_{g})^{2}=1
.

(P9)

By (
9
), we obtain

\overline{r}_{i,j}^{2}=\begin{cases}(a_{1}\dots a_{j-1})^{-2}s_{j}\overline{d}_{\set{1,\dots,j}}s_{j}\overline{d}_{\set{1,\dots,j}}^{-1}&\text{if $i=1$},\\
\big(a_{1}^{2}\dots a_{-i}^{2}a_{-i+1}\dots a_{j-1}\big)^{-2}s_{j}\overline{d}_{\set{i,\dots,j}}s_{j}\overline{d}_{\set{i,\dots,j}}^{-1}&\text{if $i\leq-1$}.\end{cases}

(P10)

We know that the
a_{i}
commute with each other and with the
\overline{d}_{i,j}
and
s
, and moreover
t_{i}*a_{i}=t_{i+1}*a_{i}=a_{i+1}
. As a consequence, we see that the
a_{i}
commute with all the
s_{j}
and the
c_{i,j}
, and that

z*a_{i}=a_{g+1-i},\qquad z_{j}*a_{i}=\begin{cases}a_{g+1-i}&\text{if $i>j$ or $j+i<g+1$},\\
a_{g-i}&\text{if $g+1-j\leq i<j$},\\
a_{i}&\text{if $i=j$}.\end{cases}

We obtain the relations in the statement, apart from the following:

(a)

\overline{r}_{i,j}*a_{j}=c_{i,j}
and
[\overline{r}_{i,j},a_{k}]=1
if
k\neq j
.

(P11)

By (
9
), we get the statement

(P12)

Again, it suffices to plug in (
9
) to get the statement.

A Schreier transversal is again given by (
8
). Indeed, notice that by (P9) and (P10)(a) we have

(12)

\overline{r}_{i,j}*c_{i,j}=\overline{r}_{i,j}^{2}*a_{j}=a_{j}.

Hence, in every word in the generators we can move all the
a_{i}
to the right, and apply the same reasoning as before. Moreover, the Schreier generators boil down to those in the statement. To see this for the
\overline{r}_{i,j}
, observe that

\overline{u_{J}\overline{r}_{1,j}^{\pm 1}}=\begin{cases}u_{J\setminus\set{j}}&\text{if}\ j\in J,\\
u_{J\Delta\set{1,\dots,j}}&\text{if}\ j\notin J,\end{cases}

where by

we denote the symmetric difference, and

\overline{u_{J}\overline{r}_{i,j}^{\pm 1}}=\begin{cases}u_{J\setminus\set{j}}&\text{if}\ j\in J,\\

if

.

Finally, the relations coming from the stabilizer of

\overline{d}_{\set{i,\dots,j}}^{2}(a_{1}^{4}\dots a_{-i}^{4}a_{-i+1}^{2}\dots a_{j}^{2})&\text{if $i\leq-1$}.\end{cases}\qed

Corollary 4.13
.

The spin handlebody group
\operatorname{Mod}(H_{g})[\phi]
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}\Braket{a_{1}^{2}}\oplus\nicefrac{{\mathbb{Z}}}{{2\mathbb{Z}}}\Braket{sa_{1}^{2}}
if
g=1
, and is generated by elements
a_{1}^{2},s,\overline{r}_{1,2}
,
t_{1}
and
u:=t_{1}\cdots t_{g-1}
if
g\geq 2
.

Proof.

The expression for
g=1
is clear. If
g>2
, the subgroup of
\operatorname{Mod}(H_{g})[\phi]
generated by
a_{1}^{2},s,\overline{r}_{1,2},t_{1}
and
u
contains all the
t_{i}
since
u*t_{i}=t_{i+1}
by
(A3)
, hence all the
a_{i}^{2}
and the
\overline{d}_{i,j}
by
(A4)
,
(A7)
,
(A8)
and
(H6)
. Since all the
k_{j}
and
s_{j}
are equal to products of generators
\overline{d}_{i,j}
,
t_{k}
and
s
,
(H7)
implies that all the
\overline{r}_{i,j}
are products of the elements in the statement. ∎

Corollary 4.14
.

The abelianization of the spin handlebody group is the following:

H_{1}(\operatorname{Mod}(H_{g})[\phi];\mathbb{Z})\cong\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}\oplus\nicefrac{{\mathbb{Z}}}{{2\mathbb{Z}}}&\text{if $g=1$},\\
\mathbb{Z}\oplus\nicefrac{{\mathbb{Z}}}{{2\mathbb{Z}}}\oplus\nicefrac{{\mathbb{Z}}}{{2\mathbb{Z}}}&\text{if $g=2$},\\
\nicefrac{{\mathbb{Z}}}{{2\mathbb{Z}}}&\text{if $g\geq 3$}.\end{cases}

Proof.

In the abelianization, all the
a_{i}^{2}
become equal to an element
y
by
(A7)
, all the
\overline{d}_{i,j}
with
i+j\neq 0
become equal to an element
x
by
(A8)
and all the
t_{i}
become equal to an element
t
by
(A3)
. Moreover, all the
\overline{d}_{-i,i}
are equal by
(A8)
, and by
(A4)
we get
\overline{d}_{-1,1}=s^{2}y
.

By
(H5)
a) and
(A4)
, we have
y=x^{-2}
and
t^{2}=x^{2}
. Moreover, by
(H6)
all the
\overline{r}_{i,j}
become equal to products of
t
and
x
. Now, by
(H4)
and
(H6)
we get
(tx)^{2}=\overline{r}_{1,2}^{2}=x^{2}s^{2}
, hence
s^{2}=x^{2}
. This shows that the abelianization is generated by
t
,
x
and
s
.

If
g=2
, the other relations become superfluous.

If
g\geq 3
, we obtain
t=x^{3}s
as a consequence of
(H7)
for
\overline{r}_{1,3}
and
(H6)
. Moreover, from
(H5)
f) for
i=1
and
j=3
we obtain
x=1
, and this implies that
s^{2}=1
. ∎

5.
Passing to Dehn twist generators

In this section, we apply Tietze moves to the presentation of ?THM?
to find a presentation where all the generators are admissible twists.

5.1.
Fake 3-chains

By a theorem of Gervais
[
8
]
, every relation in the mapping class group can be written in terms of braids,
3
-chains and lanterns. It is easy to see that a single
3
-chain cannot involve only admissible twists. However, this can be fixed via some lantern substitutions. We call the result a
fake
3
-chain
.

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}
be a
3
-chain on a spin surface
\Sigma_{g}
,
g\geq 3
. If
\phi(\gamma_{i})=1
for some
i
, it is possible to construct an admissible relation from the
3
-chain relation
C(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3})
by exactly
6
lantern substitutions (and various braid substitutions).

Proof.

First of all, we reduce to two basic cases. We will often apply tacitly braid substitutions. If
\phi(\gamma_{1})=1
or
\phi(\gamma_{3})=1
, we can assume by symmetry that
\phi(\gamma_{1})=1
. If
\phi(\gamma_{2})=1
, we have

t_{\gamma_{1}}t_{\gamma_{2}}t_{\gamma_{3}}=t_{\gamma_{2}}t_{\gamma_{2}}^{-1}t_{\gamma_{1}}t_{\gamma_{2}}t_{\gamma_{3}}=t_{\gamma_{2}}t_{t_{\gamma_{2}}^{-1}(\gamma_{1})}t_{\gamma_{3}},

so
C(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3})
is equivalent modulo braids to
C(t_{\gamma_{2}}^{-1}(\gamma_{1}),\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3})
. Hence, also in this case we can assume that
\phi(\gamma_{1})=1
. This allows us to choose freely the value of
\phi(\gamma_{2})
by twisting along
\gamma_{1}
.

If
\phi(\gamma_{3})=0
, the two boundary components
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}
of a neighborhood of the
3
-chain are both admissible. Assume that
\phi(\gamma_{2})=0
. Notice that we can rewrite the
3
-chain relation as

t_{\gamma_{1}}^{2}\,t_{\gamma_{2}}t_{\gamma_{1}}^{2}t_{\gamma_{1}}\,t_{\gamma_{3}}t_{\gamma_{2}}t_{\gamma_{1}}^{2}t_{\gamma_{2}}t_{\gamma_{3}}=t_{\delta_{1}}t_{\delta_{2}}.

By (
2
) we can apply two lantern substitutions for each occurrence of
t_{\gamma_{1}}^{2}
to get an admissible relation.

If
\phi(\gamma_{3})=1
, assume that
\phi(\gamma_{2})=1
. We can rewrite the
3
-chain relation as

(t_{\gamma_{2}}t_{\gamma_{3}}t_{\gamma_{1}}t_{\gamma_{2}})^{2}=t_{\delta_{1}}t_{\gamma_{1}}^{-1}t_{\gamma_{3}}^{-1}\,t_{\delta_{2}}t_{\gamma_{1}}^{-1}t_{\gamma_{3}}^{-1}.

Note that on the right hand side there are two fundamental multitwists, which can be made admissible via a lantern substitution each by (
1
). For the left hand side, notice that

(13)

t_{\gamma_{2}}t_{\gamma_{3}}t_{\gamma_{1}}t_{\gamma_{2}}=t_{t_{\gamma_{1}}(\gamma_{2})}t_{\gamma_{1}}^{2}t_{t_{\gamma_{3}}(\gamma_{2})}.

Using again (
2
) we conclude.

Observe that in both cases all lanterns have the same sign. ∎

We work out an example in detail.

Example 5.2
.

Consider the
3
-chain
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}
in Figure
28
. We have the following lantern relators:

\displaystyle L_{1}:=t_{y_{1}}t_{z_{1}}t_{\delta_{1}}t_{\gamma_{1}}^{-1}t_{\gamma_{3}}^{-1}t_{\varepsilon_{1}}^{-1}t_{\varepsilon_{2}}^{-1},

\displaystyle L_{2}:=t_{y_{2}}t_{z_{2}}t_{\delta_{2}}t_{\gamma_{1}}^{-1}t_{\gamma_{3}}^{-1}t_{\varepsilon_{1}}^{-1}t_{\varepsilon_{3}}^{-1},

\displaystyle L_{3}:=t_{y_{3}}t_{z_{3}}t_{\gamma_{3}}t_{\gamma_{1}}^{-1}t_{\delta_{2}}^{-1}t_{y_{1}}^{-1}t_{\varepsilon_{1}}^{-1}.

Here,
z_{1},z_{2}
and
z_{3}
can be determined from the other curves, and are easily seen to be admissible. Taking the product of the inverses of
L_{2}
and
L_{3}
, we get the relator

A:=t_{\varepsilon_{3}}t_{\varepsilon_{1}}t_{z_{2}}^{-1}t_{y_{2}}^{-1}t_{\gamma_{1}}^{2}t_{z_{3}}^{-1}t_{y_{3}}^{-1}t_{\varepsilon_{1}}t_{y_{2}}.

Since
L_{1}
,
L_{2}
and
A
contain as subwords
t_{\delta_{1}}t_{\gamma_{1}}^{-1}t_{\gamma_{3}}^{-1}
,

and
t_{\gamma_{1}}^{2}
respectively, and all the other twists that appear are admissible, we can plug some conjugates of
L_{1}
,
L_{2}
and
A^{-1}
in
C:=C(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3})
so that all non-admissible twist get canceled out, and we get the fake
3
-chain relator

F:=t_{\varepsilon_{1}}^{-1}t_{\varepsilon_{2}}^{-1}t_{y_{1}}t_{z_{1}}\,\big(t_{t_{\gamma_{1}}(\gamma_{2})}t_{y_{2}}t_{z_{2}}t_{\varepsilon_{1}}^{-1}t_{\varepsilon_{3}}^{-1}t_{y_{2}}^{-1}t_{\varepsilon_{1}}^{-1}t_{y_{3}}t_{z_{3}}t_{t_{\gamma_{3}}(\gamma_{2})}\big)^{2}\,t_{\varepsilon_{1}}^{-1}t_{\varepsilon_{3}}^{-1}t_{y_{2}}t_{z_{2}}.

We are now ready to state the presentation. We will use the symbol

.

\overline{d}_{i,j}:=\begin{cases}\big(t_{i-1}t_{i-2}\dots t_{1}t_{j-1}t_{j-2}\dots t_{2}\big)*\overline{d}_{1,2}&\text{if $i>0$},\\
\big(t_{-i-1}^{-1}\dots t_{1}^{-1}s_{1}^{-1}t_{j-1}\dots t_{2}\big)*\overline{d}_{1,2}&\text{if $0<-i<j$},\\

•

\overline{d}_{\set{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}}:=(\overline{d}_{i_{1},i_{2}}\overline{d}_{i_{1},i_{3}}\dots\overline{d}_{i_{1},i_{n}}\overline{d}_{i_{2},i_{3}}\dots\overline{d}_{i_{2},i_{n}}\dots\overline{d}_{i_{n-1},i_{n}})

•

\overline{r}_{i,j}:=b_{j}a_{j}^{2}\overline{d}_{\set{i,\dots,j}}b_{j}

Theorem 5.6
.

If
g\geq 4
, the spin mapping class group
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g}^{1})[\phi]
admits a presentation with generators
b_{1},\dots,b_{g}
,
\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{g-1}
,
\eta_{2},\dots,\eta_{g}
and the following relations:

(S1)

(S4)

(16)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\overline{r}_{i,j}^{2}&=a_{1}^{-4}\dots a_{-i}^{-4}a_{-i+1}^{-2}\dots a_{j}^{-2}\overline{d}_{\set{i,\dots,j}}^{-2}\cdot\\
&\qquad\qquad\cdot\overline{d}_{\set{i,\dots,j-1}}\big((t_{j-1}\dots t_{-i+1})*\overline{d}_{\set{i-1,\dots,j}}\big)\end{split}

Remark 5.7
.

Relation

is almost a restatement of

. However, the conjugates of the elements on the right hand side are often familiar mapping classes. For example, the relation
11

becomes

We are going to prove ?THM?
by applying Tietze moves to the presentation of ?THM?
. First of all, we add to the presentation of ?THM?
the following generators:

(18)

\displaystyle b_{k+1}:=(t_{k}\overline{d}_{k,k+1}^{-1})*b_{k},

(19)

\displaystyle\xi_{k}:=\overline{d}_{k,k+1}^{-1}*b_{k},\quad\eta_{k+1}:=t_{k}^{-1}*b_{k}.

Notice that in this enlarged presentation, the relations of ?THM?
hold true by ?THM?
. This implies that the

generate

and
t_{i-1}*a_{i+1}^{2}=a_{i+1}^{2}
follow from
(E1)
and induction, so we get

R_{2}(a_{i}^{2},a_{i+1}^{2})=t_{i-1}*R_{2}(a_{i-1}^{2},a_{i+1}^{2})

and the other relations follow as in the case
i=1
. ∎

From
(E1)
we also obtain relations
R_{4}(a_{i}^{2},E_{i-1})
,
R_{4}(a_{i}^{2},E_{i})
,
R_{4}(a_{i}^{2},N_{i})
,
R_{4}(a_{i}^{2},N_{i+1})
and
R_{2}(a_{i}^{2},E_{j})
,
R_{2}(a_{i}^{2},N_{j+1})
for every
j\neq i,i-1
. It is also useful to observe that

(20)

t_{i}=E_{i}a_{i+1}^{2}N_{i+1}.

Indeed, by
(E3)
and
R_{4}(a_{i}^{2},N_{i+1})
we have

E_{i}a_{i+1}^{2}N_{i+1}=E_{i}t_{i}^{-1}a_{i}^{2}t_{i}N_{i+1}=a_{i}^{-2}N_{i+1}^{-1}a_{i}^{2}N_{i+1}a_{i}^{2}N_{i+1}E_{i}=N_{i+1}a_{i}^{2}E_{i}.

(5)

[\overline{d}_{1,2},t_{j}]=1
for all
j\neq 2
and
[\overline{d}_{i,j},a_{k}^{2}]=1
for all
i,j,k
.

Proof.

We first show that
[\overline{d}_{1,2},t_{1}]=1
. By
(S8)
, we have
\overline{d}_{1,2}*N_{2}=a_{2}^{-2}*E_{1}
. Moreover, by (
18
), we have
\overline{d}_{1,2}*b_{2}=t_{1}*b_{1}
. Now, using ?THM?
(iv)
, we get

(21)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\overline{d}_{1,2}*E_{1}&=(t_{1}b_{1}^{-1}t_{1}^{-1})*b_{1}=(N_{2}a_{1}^{2}E_{1}b_{1}^{-1}E_{1}^{-1}a_{1}^{-2}N_{2}^{-1})*b_{1}=\\
&=(N_{2}a_{1}^{2}b_{1}^{-1}a_{1}^{-2})*\eta_{2}=(N_{2}a_{1}^{2})*N_{2}=a_{1}^{-2}*N_{2}.\end{split}

As a consequence,

\displaystyle\overline{d}_{1,2}*t_{1}

\displaystyle=a_{2}^{-2}E_{1}a_{2}^{2}N_{2}a_{1}^{2}=a_{2}^{-2}E_{1}t_{1}a_{1}^{2}t_{1}^{-1}N_{2}a_{1}^{2}=

\displaystyle=a_{2}^{-2}N_{2}E_{1}a_{1}^{2}E_{1}a_{1}^{2}E_{1}^{-1}=a_{2}^{-2}N_{2}a_{1}^{2}E_{1}a_{1}^{2}=a_{2}^{-2}t_{1}a_{1}^{2}=t_{1}.

Since from
(S10)
we have
[\overline{d}_{1,2},a_{3}^{2}]=1
, the rest then follows from
4
and ?THM?
(iv)
. ∎

As a consequence of
5
and (
18
), we see that

(22)

\overline{d}_{1,2}*b_{2}=t_{1}*b_{1}=(b_{1}^{-1}\eta_{2}a_{1}^{-2})*b_{1}=s_{1}^{-1}*\eta_{2}.

(6)

t_{i}*t_{i+1}=t_{i+1}^{-1}*t_{i}
for all
i
,
[t_{i},t_{k}]=1
if
\lvert i-k\rvert>1
and
[t_{i},s_{j}]=1
for
i\neq j
.

Proof.

All the relations follow easily from
(E1)
and
4
except for the first one, which can be rewritten using (
20
) as

(t_{i}*N_{i+2})a_{i}^{2}(t_{i}*E_{i+1})=(t_{i+1}^{-1}*N_{i+1})a_{i}^{2}(t_{i+1}^{-1}*E_{i}).

We are going to prove that
t_{i}*N_{i+2}=t_{i+1}^{-1}*N_{i+1}
and
t_{i}*E_{i+1}=t_{i+1}^{-1}*E_{i}
. We have
t_{i}*N_{i+2}=(N_{i+1}a_{i}^{2}E_{i})*N_{i+2}=N_{i+1}*N_{i+2}
and

\displaystyle t_{i+1}^{-1}*N_{i+1}

\displaystyle=(E_{i+1}^{-1}a_{i+1}^{-2}N_{i+2}^{-1})*N_{i+1}=

\displaystyle=(E_{i+1}^{-1}N_{i+1}a_{i}^{2}E_{i}a_{i}^{2}E_{i}^{-1}a_{i}^{-2}N_{i+1}^{-1}N_{i+1})*N_{i+2}=N_{i+1}*N_{i+2}.

For the other equality, we have

\displaystyle t_{i+1}^{-1}*E_{i}

\displaystyle=(E_{i+1}^{-1}a_{i+1}^{-2})*E_{i}=(E_{i+1}^{-1}E_{i}^{-1}a_{i}^{-2}N_{i+1}^{-1}a_{i}^{-2}N_{i+1}a_{i}^{2}E_{i})*E_{i}=

\displaystyle=(E_{i+1}^{-1}E_{i}^{-1}N_{i+1}a_{i}^{-2}N_{i+1}^{-1})*E_{i}=(N_{i+1}E_{i+1}^{-1}E_{i}^{-1}a_{i}^{-2})*E_{i}=

\displaystyle=(N_{i+1}E_{i+1}^{-1}a_{i}^{2})*E_{i}=t_{i}*E_{i+1}.\qed

(7)

For
i>0
, we have

\displaystyle\overline{d}_{i,i+1}=(t_{i-1}t_{i}t_{i-2}t_{i-1}\dots t_{1}t_{2})*\overline{d}_{1,2},

\displaystyle\overline{d}_{-i-1,-i}=(t_{i-1}^{-1}t_{i}^{-1}t_{i-2}^{-1}t_{i-1}^{-1}\dots t_{1}^{-1}t_{2}^{-1})*\overline{d}_{-2,-1}.

As a consequence, we have

\overline{d}_{i,i+1}=(t_{i-1}t_{i})*\overline{d}_{i-1,i},\quad\overline{d}_{-i-1,-i}=(t_{i-1}^{-1}t_{i}^{-1})*\overline{d}_{-i,-i+1}

for
i>1
, and
t_{k}*\overline{d}_{i,i+1}=\overline{d}_{i,i+1}
if
\lvert k-i\rvert\neq 1
.

Proof.

This follows easily from (
15
),
5
and
6
. ∎

Recall that
H_{2}:=\eta_{3}\eta_{2}b_{1}a_{1}^{2}b_{1}\eta_{2}\eta_{3}
.

(8)

H_{2}*b_{2}=H_{2}^{-1}*b_{2}
and
[H_{2}*b_{2},b_{2}]=1
.

Proof.

Applying repeatedly relations
(S1)
and
(E1)
, we see that
H_{2}
and
b_{2}
both commute with
a_{1}^{2}(b_{1}a_{1}^{2}b_{1})(\eta_{2}b_{1}a_{1}^{2}b_{1}\eta_{2})
. Then, using
(S4)
we can do the same proof as
[
31
, (9)]
. ∎

(9)

s_{1}t_{1}s_{1}t_{1}=t_{1}s_{1}t_{1}s_{1}
.

Proof.

We have to prove that
(s_{1}t_{1})*s_{1}=t_{1}^{-1}*s_{1}
. Since

(s_{1}t_{1})*a_{1}^{2}=s_{1}*a_{2}^{2}=a_{2}^{2}=t_{1}^{-1}*a_{1}^{2}

by
4
, it suffices to prove that
(st_{1})*b_{1}
is equal to
t_{1}^{-1}*b_{1}=\eta_{2}
. We have

(s_{1}t_{1})*b_{1}=(b_{1}a_{1}^{2}b_{1}b_{1}^{-1}\eta_{2}b_{1}a_{1}^{2}E_{1})*b_{1}=(b_{1}\eta_{2}a_{1}^{2}b_{1}a_{1}^{2})*b_{1}=\eta_{2}.\qed

(10)

s_{1}t_{1}s_{1}=t_{1}w_{1}=w_{1}t_{1}
, where
w_{1}:=(\eta_{2}a_{2}^{2}N_{2}a_{1}^{2}N_{2}\eta_{2})
.

Proof.

The idea is similar to that of
[
31
, (10)]
. By
(E2)
and
(E1)
, we have

t_{1}w_{1}=t_{1}\eta_{2}t_{1}^{-1}a_{1}^{2}t_{1}N_{2}a_{1}^{2}\eta_{2}b_{1}=b_{1}a_{1}^{2}t_{1}\eta_{2}b_{1}a_{1}^{2}b_{1}=b_{1}a_{1}^{2}b_{1}t_{1}b_{1}a_{1}^{2}b_{1}=s_{1}t_{1}s_{1}.

By
9
,
st_{1}s=t_{1}st_{1}st_{1}^{-1}
, so the other equality follows. ∎

(11)

t_{1}^{2}=\overline{d}_{1,2}\overline{d}_{-2,-1}
,
[\overline{d}_{1,2},\overline{d}_{-2,-1}]=1
and

\overline{d}_{-2,-1}=w_{i}^{-1}*\overline{d}_{1,2}=w_{i}*\overline{d}_{1,2}

for
i=1,2,3
, where

w_{2}:=N_{3}a_{2}^{2}N_{2}a_{1}^{2}N_{2}N_{3},\quad w_{3}:=E_{2}E_{1}a_{1}^{2}E_{1}a_{2}^{2}E_{2}.

Proof.

The first relation is
(S4)
, and the second relation follows easily. For the third one, we have
\overline{d}_{-2,-1}=(st_{1}s)^{-1}*\overline{d}_{1,2}=(t_{1}w_{1})^{-1}*\overline{d}_{1,2}=w_{1}^{-1}*\overline{d}_{1,2}
by
5
and
10
. On the other hand,

w_{1}*\overline{d}_{1,2}=w_{1}*(t_{1}^{2}\overline{d}_{-2,-1}^{-1})=t_{1}^{2}(w_{1}*\overline{d}_{-2,-1}^{-1})=t_{1}^{2}\overline{d}_{1,2}^{-1}=\overline{d}_{-2,-1}.

Conjugating by
m_{2}=(\eta_{2}\eta_{3})*b_{2}
we obtain the last relation for
i=2
. Indeed, notice first that

w_{1}=\eta_{2}N_{2}a_{1}^{2}E_{1}a_{1}^{2}E_{1}^{-1}N_{2}\eta_{2},\quad w_{2}=N_{3}N_{2}a_{1}^{2}E_{1}a_{1}^{2}E_{1}^{-1}N_{2}N_{3};

then, it suffices to apply ?THM?
(iv)
. Since
m_{2}
commutes with
\overline{d}_{1,2}
and
t_{1}
, it also commutes with
\overline{d}_{-2,-1}=t_{1}^{2}\overline{d}_{1,2}^{-1}
.

For
i=3
, we claim that
w_{3}
is the result of conjugation of
w_{2}
by

\psi:=\big((E_{2}\eta_{4}\overline{d}_{1,2})*N_{3}\big)^{-1}\cdot\eta_{4}\overline{d}_{1,2}.

First of all, by (
22
) we get
\overline{d}_{1,2}*N_{3}=(\eta_{3}s_{1}^{-1})*\eta_{2}
. This clearly implies that relations
R_{3}(\eta_{4},\overline{d}_{1,2}*N_{3})
and
R_{2}(E_{2},\overline{d}_{1,2}*N_{3})
hold. As a consequence,

\psi*N_{3}=\big(E_{2}\eta_{4}\cdot(\overline{d}_{1,2}*N_{3})^{-1}(E_{2}*\eta_{4})^{-1})\big)*(\overline{d}_{1,2}*N_{3})=(E_{2}\eta_{4}E_{2})*\eta_{4}=E_{2}.

Now, applying ?THM?
(iii)
we have

\displaystyle\overline{d}_{1,2}^{-1}*E_{2}

\displaystyle=(\eta_{4}\eta_{3}b_{3}\eta_{4}m_{4}^{-1}\eta_{4}^{-1}b_{3}^{-1}\eta_{3}^{-1}\eta_{4}^{-1}m_{4}^{-1})*\xi_{2}=(\eta_{4}m_{4}^{-1}\eta_{3}b_{3}\eta_{4}^{-1}b_{3}^{-1}\eta_{3}^{-1}\eta_{4}^{-1}m_{4}^{-1})*\xi_{2}=

\displaystyle=(\eta_{4}m_{4}^{-1}\eta_{3}\eta_{4}^{-1}\eta_{3}^{-1}b_{3}^{-1}\eta_{4}^{-1}m_{4}^{-1})*\xi_{2}=(m_{4}^{-1}\eta_{4}^{-1}m_{4}\eta_{3}^{-1}b_{3}^{-1}\eta_{4}^{-1}b_{3}\xi_{2})*m_{4}.

Since by
(S5)

\eta_{4}
commutes with
(b_{3}\xi_{2})*m_{4}
, we get

\overline{d}_{1,2}^{-1}*E_{2}=(m_{4}^{-1}\eta_{4}^{-1}m_{4}\xi_{2})*m_{4}=m_{4}^{-1}*\xi_{2}

Hence, by
(S5)
and by
(S8)
we have

\displaystyle\psi*N_{2}

\displaystyle=(E_{2}\eta_{4}\overline{d}_{1,2}\,N_{3}^{-1}\,\overline{d}_{1,2}^{-1}\eta_{4}^{-1}E_{2}^{-1}\,a_{2}^{-2})*E_{1}=(E_{2}\overline{d}_{1,2}\,N_{3}^{-1}\eta_{4}^{-1}N_{3}\,\overline{d}_{1,2}^{-1}E_{2}^{-1}\,a_{2}^{-2})*E_{1}=

\displaystyle=(\overline{d}_{1,2}\xi_{2}m_{4}\xi_{2}^{-1}\,N_{3}^{-1}\eta_{4}^{-1}N_{3}\,\xi_{2}m_{4}^{-1}\xi_{2}^{-1}\overline{d}_{1,2}^{-1}\,a_{2}^{-2})*E_{1}=

\displaystyle=(\overline{d}_{1,2}b_{2}^{-1}\xi_{2}m_{4}\,\eta_{3}^{-1}\eta_{4}^{-1}\eta_{3}\,m_{4}^{-1})*N_{2}=(\overline{d}_{1,2}b_{2}^{-1}\xi_{2})*N_{2}=a_{2}^{-2}*E_{1}.

This proves that
\psi*w_{2}=w_{3}
. Now,
\psi
is easily seen to commute with
\overline{d}_{1,2}
and
t_{1}
, hence also with
\overline{d}_{-2,-1}
. ∎

(12)

\overline{d}_{i+1,i+2}=(t_{i}^{-1}t_{i+1}^{-1})*\overline{d}_{i,i+1}
for all
i=1,\dots,g-2
.

Proof.

By
7
, it suffices to prove that
(t_{i+1}t_{i}^{2}t_{i+1})*\overline{d}_{i,i+1}=\overline{d}_{i,i+1}
. We do induction on
i
. For
i=1
, notice that

t_{2}t_{1}^{2}t_{2}=N_{3}a_{2}^{2}E_{2}N_{2}a_{1}^{2}E_{1}N_{2}a_{1}^{2}E_{1}N_{3}a_{2}^{2}E_{2}=N_{3}a_{2}^{2}N_{2}a_{1}^{2}N_{2}N_{3}E_{2}E_{1}a_{1}^{2}E_{1}a_{2}^{2}E_{2}=w_{2}w_{3}.

Thus, by
11
we have
(t_{2}t_{1}^{2}t_{2})*\overline{d}_{1,2}=(w_{2}w_{3})*\overline{d}_{1,2}=w_{2}*\overline{d}_{-2,-1}=\overline{d}_{1,2}
. For the inductive step see
[
31
, (13)]
. ∎

(13)

t_{i}^{2}=\overline{d}_{i,i+1}\overline{d}_{-i-1,-i}
for all
i=1,\dots,g-1
.

Proof.

See
[
31
, (14)]
. ∎

Notice that so far we have obtained relations
(A1)
,
(A3)
,
(A4)
,
(A5)
,
(A6)
and

as consequences of

-

.

5.3.
Further relations

Now we derive relations
(A2)
and
(A8)
from
(S1)
-
(S14)
. Many steps of the proof are similar to those in
[
31
]
. Observe that the notion of symmetry considered by Wajnryb does not really apply to our context, essentially as a consequence of the asymmetry in (

). However, it is easy to adapt Wajnryb’s arguments to the extra cases.

(14)

[b_{1},\overline{d}_{-2,2}]=1
.

Proof.

As in
[
31
, (16)]
, we find that
\overline{d}_{-2,2}=a_{2}^{2}((\overline{d}_{1,2}t_{1}^{-1})*s_{1}^{2})
. We have to prove that
b_{1}
commutes with
(\overline{d}_{1,2}t_{1}^{-1})*s_{1}^{2}
. We have

(b_{1}\overline{d}_{1,2}t_{1}^{-1})*s_{1}^{2}=(\overline{d}_{1,2}\xi_{1}\,E_{1}a_{1}^{-2}N_{2}^{-1})*s^{2}=(\overline{d}_{1,2}E_{1}\,b_{2}a_{1}^{-2}N_{2}^{-1})*s^{2}=(\overline{d}_{1,2}t_{1}^{-1})*s^{2}.\qed

(15)

\xi_{k}=(b_{k+1}t_{k-1}t_{k}b_{k}^{-1})*\xi_{k-1}
and
\eta_{k+1}=(b_{k}t_{k-1}t_{k}b_{k-1}^{-1})*\eta_{k}
for all
k\geq 2
.

Proof.

The statement is equivalent to
E_{k}=(t_{k-1}t_{k})*E_{k-1}
and
N_{k+1}=(t_{k-1}t_{k})*N_{k}
. Using (
20
), we get

(t_{k-1}t_{k})*E_{k-1}=(N_{k}a_{k-1}^{2}E_{k-1}E_{k}a_{k+1}^{2}N_{k+1})*E_{k-1}=(N_{k}a_{k-1}^{2}E_{k-1}E_{k})*E_{k-1}=E_{k}.

Since
N_{i+1}=t_{i}E_{i}^{-1}a_{i}^{-2}
for all
i
, the second relation follows from
6
and the first one. ∎

(16)

\overline{d}_{i,i+1}*E_{i}=a_{i}^{-2}*N_{i+1}
and
\overline{d}_{i,i+1}*N_{i+1}=a_{i+1}^{-2}*E_{i}
for all
i
.

Proof.

We do induction on
i
. The base case is
(S8)
and (
21
). In general, by
7
and
15
, we obtain

\overline{d}_{i,i+1}*E_{i}=(t_{i-1}t_{i}\overline{d}_{i-1,i}t_{i}^{-1}t_{i-1}^{-1})*E_{i}=(t_{i-1}t_{i}\overline{d}_{i-1,i})*(E_{i-1})=(t_{i-1}t_{i}a_{i-1}^{-2})*N_{i}=a_{i}^{-2}*N_{i+1}

and

\overline{d}_{i,i+1}*N_{i+1}=(t_{i-1}t_{i}\overline{d}_{i-1,i}t_{i}^{-1}t_{i-1}^{-1})*N_{i+1}=(t_{i-1}t_{i}\overline{d}_{i-1,i})*N_{i}=(t_{i-1}t_{i}a_{i}^{-2})*E_{i-1}=a_{i+1}^{-2}*E_{i}.\qed

(17)

[s_{k},\overline{d}_{i,i+1}]=1
if
k\neq i,i+1
and
[s_{i},\overline{d}_{i,i+1}s_{i}\overline{d}_{i,i+1}]=1
.

Proof.

For the first relation, it is enough to show that
\overline{d}_{i,i+1}*b_{k}=b_{k}
if
k\neq i,i+1
. Clearly
\overline{d}_{i,i+1}
commutes with
b_{k}
for
k\geq i+2
. For the rest, we do induction on
i
. The base case is ?THM?
(iv)
. Suppose that
\overline{d}_{i-1,i}*b_{k}=b_{k}
for
k<i-1
. Then also
\overline{d}_{i,i+1}*b_{k}=b_{k}
for
k<i-1
by
7
. For
k=i-1
, it is enough to prove that
\overline{d}_{i-1,i}
commutes with
(t_{i}^{-1}t_{i-1}^{-1})*b_{i-1}
. Observe that by (
20
) we have

(t_{i}^{-1}t_{i-1}^{-1})*b_{i-1}=(N_{i+1}^{-1}a_{i+1}^{-2}E_{i}^{-1})*\eta_{i}=N_{i+1}^{-1}*\eta_{i}=(\eta_{i}\eta_{i+1})*b_{i},

so the desired relation is
(S9)
.

The second relation follows from
(S8)
. Indeed, we have

(s_{i}\overline{d}_{i,i+1})*b_{i}=(b_{i}a_{i}^{2}b_{i}a_{i}^{-2}b_{i}^{-1}a_{i}^{-2})*\xi_{i}=a_{i}^{-2}*\xi_{i}=(a_{i}^{-2}\overline{d}_{i,i+1}^{-1})*b_{i},

and then it is straightforward to conclude. ∎

(18)

[t_{j},\overline{d}_{i,i+1}t_{j}\overline{d}_{i,i+1}]=1
for
j=i\pm 1
.

Proof.

We do induction on
i
. The base case is
(S10)
. Then, as a consequence of
6
and
7
, we have

[t_{i},\overline{d}_{i+1,i+2}t_{i}\overline{d}_{i+1,i+2}]=(t_{i}t_{i+1}t_{i})*[t_{i+1},\overline{d}_{i,i+1}t_{i+1}\overline{d}_{i,i+1}]

and

[t_{i+2},\overline{d}_{i+1,i+2}t_{i+2}\overline{d}_{i+1,i+2}]=(t_{i}t_{i+1}t_{i+2})*[t_{i+1},\overline{d}_{i,i+1}t_{i+1}\overline{d}_{i,i+1}].\qed

Lemma 5.9
.

(A8)
follow from

Proof.

Now we move on to relations
(A2)
.

(19)

[\overline{d}_{i,j},\overline{d}_{-1,1}]=1
if
i,j\neq\pm 1
, and
[\overline{d}_{i,j},\overline{d}_{k,k+1}]=1

Lemma 5.10

(
[
31
, Lemma 34]
)
.

The following relations hold:

(a)

t_{k}^{-1}\overline{d}_{k,k+1}
commutes with
\overline{d}_{i,j}
if
i,j\neq\pm k,\pm(k+1)
;

(b)

t_{k}^{-1}\overline{d}_{k,k+1}
commutes with
\overline{d}_{k,k+1}
and
\overline{d}_{-k-1,-k}
;

(c)

(t_{k}^{-1}\overline{d}_{k,k+1})*\overline{d}_{i,\pm k}=\overline{d}_{i,\pm k\pm 1}
if
i\neq-k-1
and
i+k\neq 0
;

(d)

(t_{k}^{-1}\overline{d}_{k,k+1})*\overline{d}_{\pm k,j}=\overline{d}_{\pm k\pm 1,j}
if
j\neq k+1
and
-k+j\neq 0
;

(e)

(t_{k}^{-1}\overline{d}_{k,k+1})*\overline{d}_{-k,k}=\overline{d}_{-k-1,k+1}
;

Lemma 5.11
.

Relations
(A2)
follow from
(S1)
-
(S14)
.

Proof.

See
[
31
, (21), (22), (23), (24)]
. ∎

To conclude the proof of ?THM?
, we only have to deal with the relations coming from the action of
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g,1})[\phi]
on the edges of
X_{g}
.

We start from relations
10
. Notice that we have

(t_{1}s_{1}t_{1})*b_{1}=(t_{1}b_{1}\eta_{2}a_{1}^{2}b_{1}a_{1}^{2})*b_{1}=(t_{1})*\eta_{2}=b_{1}

and

\overline{d}_{-1,1}\overline{d}_{-1,2}\overline{d}_{1,2}a_{1}^{2}=a_{1}^{4}s_{1}\overline{d}_{1,2}s_{1}\overline{d}_{1,2},

so by
(S8)
and
17
we get

(a_{1}^{4}s_{1}\overline{d}_{1,2}s_{1}\overline{d}_{1,2})*b_{1}=(a_{1}^{2}s_{1}\overline{d}_{1,2})*\xi_{1}=(a_{1}^{2}s_{1})*b_{1}=b_{1}.

This proves
10a)
, and
10b)
follows immediately. It is easy to see that relations
10c)
follow from the above. For
10d)
, notice that the first relation is equivalent to

(23)

[a_{j}^{2}\overline{d}_{i,\dots,j},\overline{r}_{i,j}]=1,

which is the first half of
(S13)
. We will deal with the last part of
10d)
later on.

Consider now relation
9
. We deal only with the case

, as the other is similar. By (

b_{j}^{+2}\overline{r}_{i,j}\overline{d}_{\set{i,\dots,j}}a_{j}^{2}b_{j}^{-2}\overline{r}_{i,j}=b_{j}^{+2}\overline{d}_{\set{i,\dots,j}}a_{j}^{2}\overline{r}_{i,j}b_{j}^{-2}\overline{r}_{i,j}=b_{j}^{+2}\overline{d}_{\set{i,\dots,j}}a_{j}^{2}b_{j}\overline{d}_{\set{i,\dots,j}}a_{j}^{2}\overline{d}_{\set{i,\dots,j}}a_{j}^{2}b_{j}.

Hence, writing out the definition of
s_{j}
, the relation becomes

b_{j}\overline{d}_{\set{i,\dots,j}}a_{j}^{2}b_{j}a_{j}^{2}\overline{d}_{\set{i,\dots,j}}^{2}=a_{1}^{-4}\dots a_{-i}^{-4}a_{-i+1}^{-2}\dots a_{j-1}^{-2}a_{j}^{2}b_{j}a_{j}^{2}\overline{d}_{\set{i,\dots,j}}b_{j}.

Applying once more (
23
), we obtain the second half of
(S13)
.

Consider now relation
10
10d)
for
i<0
. Applying
9
, we have

\displaystyle s_{j}a_{j}^{-2}\overline{d}_{\set{1,\dots,j}}^{-1}\overline{r}_{i,j}^{-1}b_{j}^{-2}a_{j}^{2}s_{j}\overline{d}_{\set{i,\dots,j}}b_{j}^{2}\overline{r}_{i,j}

\displaystyle=a_{1}^{4}\dots a_{-i}^{4}a_{-i+1}^{2}\dots a_{j-1}^{2}s_{j}b_{j}^{-2}\overline{r}_{i,j}s_{j}^{-1}b_{j}^{2}\overline{r}_{i,j}=

\displaystyle=a_{1}^{4}\dots a_{-i}^{4}a_{-i+1}^{2}\dots a_{j-1}^{2}\overline{r}_{i,j}^{2}.

The relation becomes

\overline{r}_{i,j}^{2}=a_{1}^{-4}\dots a_{-i}^{-4}a_{-i+1}^{-2}\dots a_{j}^{-2}\overline{d}_{\set{i,\dots,j}}^{-2}\cdot\overline{d}_{\set{i,\dots,j-1}}\big((t_{j-1}\dots t_{-i+1})*\overline{d}_{\set{i-1,\dots,j}}\big),

which is

.

For the triangles, we have already observed that relations

Relation

:

(a_{1}^{2}b_{1}t_{1}\overline{d}_{1,2}^{-1}a_{1}^{2}b_{1}t_{1}\overline{d}_{-2,-1}^{-1})^{2}=(a_{1}^{2}b_{1}a_{2}^{2}b_{2}t_{1}\overline{d}_{1,2}^{-1}t_{1}\overline{d}_{-2,-1}^{-1})^{2}=(a_{1}^{2}b_{1}a_{2}^{2}b_{2})^{2}=a_{1}^{2}s_{1}a_{2}^{2}s_{2}.

Observe that applying ?THM?

\displaystyle s_{2}b_{2}^{-2}\overline{r}_{1,2}\overline{d}_{-2,-1}\overline{d}_{1,2}^{-1}b_{1}t_{1}a_{1}^{2}b_{1}\overline{d}_{-2,-1}^{-3}t_{1}b_{1}t_{1}b_{1}\overline{d}_{\set{-2,-1,1,2}}^{-1}=

\displaystyle\quad=b_{2}a_{2}^{2}\overline{d}_{1,2}a_{2}^{2}b_{2}\overline{d}_{-2,-1}\overline{d}_{1,2}^{-1}b_{1}t_{1}a_{1}^{2}b_{1}\overline{d}_{1,2}t_{1}^{-1}\overline{d}_{-2,-1}^{-2}t_{1}\eta_{2}b_{1}\overline{d}_{\set{-2,-1,1,2}}^{-1}=

\displaystyle\quad=b_{2}a_{2}^{2}\overline{d}_{1,2}a_{2}^{2}b_{2}\overline{d}_{-2,-1}\overline{d}_{1,2}^{-1}b_{1}\overline{d}_{1,2}a_{2}^{2}b_{2}\overline{d}_{-2,-1}^{-2}t_{1}\eta_{2}b_{1}\overline{d}_{\set{-2,-1,1,2}}^{-1}=

\displaystyle\quad=b_{2}a_{2}^{2}\overline{d}_{1,2}a_{2}^{2}b_{2}\overline{d}_{-2,-1}\xi_{1}a_{2}^{2}b_{2}t_{1}^{-1}\overline{d}_{1,2}\overline{d}_{-2,-1}^{-1}\eta_{2}b_{1}\overline{d}_{\set{-2,-1,1,2}}^{-1}=

Now, applying

\displaystyle\quad=b_{2}a_{2}^{2}\overline{d}_{1,2}a_{2}^{2}b_{2}a_{2}^{2}b_{2}\overline{d}_{-2,-1}\overline{d}_{-1,1,2}\overline{d}_{\set{-2,-1,1,2}}^{-1}b_{2}=

\displaystyle\quad=b_{2}a_{2}^{2}\overline{d}_{1,2}b_{2}a_{2}^{2}b_{2}a_{2}^{2}\overline{d}_{1,2}(t_{1}*\overline{d}_{-1,1,2})^{-1}b_{2}=\overline{r}_{1,2}a_{2}^{2}\overline{r}_{1,2}(t_{1}*\overline{d}_{-1,1,2})^{-1}.

Observe that by

we have

\overline{r}_{1,2}a_{2}^{2}\overline{r}_{1,2}=\overline{r}_{1,2}a_{2}^{2}\overline{d}_{1,2}\overline{d}_{1,2}^{-1}\overline{r}_{1,2}=\overline{r}_{1,2}^{2}a_{1}^{2}a_{2}^{2}\overline{d}_{1,2}^{2},

and by

Finally,

6.
The case of closed surfaces

In this section, we finally determine a finite presentation of the spin mapping class group of a closed surface. There is a standard procedure to relate the mapping class group of
\Sigma_{g}^{1}
and that of
\Sigma_{g}
: first we cap off the boundary component
\partial
with a once-marked disk, obtaining a surface
\Sigma_{g,1}
, and then we forget the marked point
p
. These two steps correspond to two well-known exact sequences of groups (see
[
7
, Proposition 3.19 and Theorem 4.6]
):

\displaystyle 1\longrightarrow\Braket{t_{\partial}}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g}^{1})\overset{Cap}{\longrightarrow}\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g,1})\longrightarrow 1,

\displaystyle 1\longrightarrow\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g},p)\overset{Push}{\longrightarrow}\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g,1})\overset{Forget}{\longrightarrow}\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g})\longrightarrow 1.

The second sequence is known as the Birman exact sequence. By
[
7
, Fact 4.7]
, the kernel of
Forget
is generated by mapping classes of the form
t_{\gamma}t_{\gamma^{\prime}}^{-1}
, where
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
bound an annulus containing
p
, i.e. Birman’s “spin maps”
[
4
]
.

Consider now an even spin structure
\phi
on
\Sigma_{g}^{1}
. By our assumptions,
\phi(\partial)=1
, so
\Sigma_{g,1}
and
\Sigma_{g}
inherit well-defined spin structures, which we still denote by
\phi
. Moreover, it is clear that the maps
Cap
and
Forget
restrict to surjections between the stabilizer subgroups. Since all the mapping classes in both kernels are easily seen to preserve
\phi
, we get analogous exact sequences

(24)

\displaystyle 1\longrightarrow\Braket{t_{\partial}}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g}^{1})[\phi]\overset{Cap}{\longrightarrow}\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g,1})[\phi]\longrightarrow 1,

(25)

\displaystyle 1\longrightarrow\pi_{1}(\Sigma_{g},p)\overset{Push}{\longrightarrow}\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g,1})[\phi]\overset{Forget}{\longrightarrow}\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g})[\phi]\longrightarrow 1.

We can now obtain a presentation for
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g})[\phi]
, where
\phi
is the even spin structure of the preceding sections.

Theorem 6.1
.

The spin mapping class group
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g})[\phi]
admits a presentation with generators
b_{1},\dots,b_{g}
,
\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{g-1}
,
\eta_{2},\dots,\eta_{g}
and relations
(S1)
-
(S14)
and the following:

(15)

\overline{d}_{\set{-g,\dots,g}}a_{1}^{2}\dots a_{g}^{2}=1
;

(16)

\overline{d}_{\set{-g,\dots,-1}}=\overline{d}_{\set{1,\dots,g}}
.

Proof.

Notice that
\partial=\delta_{\set{-g,\dots,g}}
in the notation of Figure
23
. Hence,

t_{\partial}=d_{\set{-g,\dots,g}}=\overline{d}_{\set{-g,\dots,g}}a_{1}^{2}\dots a_{g}^{2}

by (
11
), and modding out
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g}^{1})[\phi]
by
15
we get
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g,1})[\phi]
by (
24
).

In order to obtain
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g})[\phi]
, we have to mod out by the subgroup generated by mapping classes
t_{\gamma}t_{\gamma^{\prime}}^{-1}
as above. Clearly, the action of
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g}^{1})[\phi]
on these elements has two orbits, according to the spin value of
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
. A couple of
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}
with spin value
1
is given by
\delta_{\set{-g,\dots,-1}}
and
\delta_{\set{1,\dots,g}}
, and relation
16
implies that

d_{\set{1,\dots,g}}d_{\set{-g,\dots,-1}}^{-1}=\overline{d}_{\set{1,\dots,g}}\overline{d}_{\set{-g,\dots,-1}}^{-1}=1.

A couple of admissible
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}
is given by the curves
\beta_{g}
and
\beta_{g}^{\prime}
of Figure
31
. Notice that

\beta_{g}^{\prime}=\big(\overline{d}_{\set{-g,\dots,-1}}^{-1}\overline{d}_{\set{1,\dots,g}}\big)(\beta_{g})=\beta_{g}

by
16
. This concludes the proof. ∎

Remark 6.2
.

By
[
8
]
, all the relations appearing in ?THM?
and ?THM?
can be expressed in terms of braids, chains and lanterns. This is obvious for
(S1)
and
(S2)
. It follows from the remarks after (
3
) that
(S3)
is the combination of two positive
7
-chains and two negative
6
-chains. Moreover,
(S5)
-
(S11)
are Artin relations, so they are clearly products of lanterns with total exponent zero modulo braids.

For the remaining relations, recall from ?THM?
that if
\lvert I\rvert=n
, then
\overline{d}_{I}
can be written as a product of
\overline{d}_{i,j}
using exactly
(n-1)(n-2)/2
lanterns. Since each
\overline{d}_{i,j}
can be written as a product of admissible twists by taking the product with a negative lantern, we see that
\overline{d}_{I}
can be factored using
1-\lvert I\rvert
lanterns, counted with multiplicity. For example, according to ?THM?
a fake
3
-chain involves a
3
-chain and
6
lanterns. This is clear for the first two relations of
(S4)
. For (
16
), we have
2(3-(j-i))
lanterns in the left hand side, and
-2(j-i)
lanterns on the right hand side, so this remains true.

Counting the lanterns with signs in this way, we see that every relation appearing in
(S12)
-
11
and
16
involves a total amount of zero lanterns. Notice that (
17
) comes from a lantern in
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g})
, so it involves an extra (positive) lantern.

For
16
, we obtain a total of
1-2g+2g=1
lantern. Notice that since the curve
\delta_{\set{-g,\dots,g}}
bounds a disk on
\Sigma_{g}
, the remaining lantern has a trivial boundary component.

Corollary 6.3

(Randal-Williams
[
23
]
, Sierra
[
26
]
)
.

The abelianization of the even spin mapping class group is
\nicefrac{{\mathbb{Z}}}{{4\mathbb{Z}}}
for
g\geq 4
.

Proof.

Take the presentation of ?THM?
and add all commutators. Relation
(S1)
implies that all the generators become equal to some
x
. Relations
(S5)
-
(S11)
become trivial. From relation
(S2)
we find
28x=0
, while relation
(S3)
gives
56x=0
.

For the remaining relations, we have to write the classes of
s_{i}
,
t_{i}
,
a_{i}^{2}
,
\overline{d}_{I}
and
\overline{r}_{i,j}
in the abelianization. By ?THM?
,
\overline{d}_{1,2}
and
a_{1}^{2}
become zero, and so do their conjugates
a_{i}^{2}
and
\overline{d}_{i,j}
with
i\neq j
. As a consequence, we get
s_{j}=t_{j}=\overline{r}_{1,j}=2x
for all
j
. Finally,
\overline{d}_{-1,1}
and its conjugates
\overline{d}_{-j,j}
become equal to
4x
. Hence,
\overline{d}_{\set{i,\dots,j}}=4\,\lvert i\rvert\,x
and
\overline{r}_{i,j}=4\,\lvert i\rvert\,x+2
for
i<0
and
j>-i
.

Hence, relations
(S4)
give
4x=0
, while
(S13)
-
16
do not give other restriction to the order of
x
. This concludes the proof. ∎

6.1.
Surfaces of low genus

In this section, we give presentations of the even spin mapping class group for surfaces of genus
1
,
2
and
3
. We start from the case of the torus, where a presentation can be derived by hand.

Proposition 6.4
.

The even spin mapping class group of a torus is given by

\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{1})[\phi]=\Braket{a^{2},b|(a^{2}b)^{2}=(ba^{2})^{2},\,(a^{2}b)^{4}=1},

where
a^{2}=t_{\alpha_{1}}^{2}
and
b=t_{\beta_{1}}
in the notation of Figure
20
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{1})[\phi]
is generated by
a^{2}
and
b
; then the statement follows easily using the Nielsen-Schreier method.

An element
\varphi\in\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{1})
can be represented by a word
a^{k_{1}}b^{\ell_{1}}\dots a^{k_{n}}b^{\ell_{n}}
, for some integers
k_{1},\ell_{1},\dots,k_{n},\ell_{n}
. If
\varphi
preserves
\phi
, we prove that
\varphi
can be written as a product of
a^{2}
and
b
by induction on
n
. The base case is ?THM?
(b). For the inductive step, observe that if
\varphi
preserves
\phi
then also
\varphi b^{-\ell_{n}}=a^{k_{1}}b^{\ell_{1}}\dots b^{\ell_{n-1}}a^{k_{n}}
does. If
k_{n}
is even, we conclude by induction. Otherwise,

\varphi b^{-\ell_{n}}a^{-k_{n}+1}=a^{k_{1}}b^{\ell_{1}}\dots a^{k_{n-1}}b^{\ell_{n-1}}abb^{-1}=a^{k_{1}}b^{\ell_{1}}\dots a^{k_{n-1}+1}ba^{\ell_{n-1}}b^{-1}

preserves
\phi
. If
\ell_{n-1}
is even, we conclude by induction. Otherwise,

\varphi b^{-\ell_{n}}a^{-k_{n}+1}ba^{-\ell_{n-1}+1}=a^{k_{1}}b^{\ell_{1}}\dots b^{\ell_{n-2}}a^{k_{n-1}+1}ba=a^{k_{1}}b^{\ell_{1}}\dots b^{\ell_{n-2}+1}ab^{k_{n-1}+1}

preserves
\phi
, and we conclude by induction. ∎

A similar reasoning can be done for genus
2
, but in this case we apply ?THM?
and ?THM?
.

Corollary 6.5
.

The even spin mapping class group
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{2})[\phi]
admits a presentation with generators
a_{1}^{2}
,
b_{1}
,
t_{1}
,
\overline{d}_{1,2}
, and the following relations:

(i)

[a_{1}^{2},t_{1}*a_{1}^{2}]=1
,
[a_{1}^{2},\overline{d}_{1,2}]=1
,
[t_{1}*a_{1}^{2},b_{1}]=1
and
[b_{1},(t_{1}\overline{d}_{1,2}^{-1})*b_{1}]=1
;

(ii)

R_{4}(a_{1}^{2},b_{1})
and
R_{4}(b_{1}a_{1}^{2}b_{1},t_{1})
;

(iii)

t_{1}^{2}=\overline{d}_{1,2}^{2}
;

(iv)

R_{2}(t_{1},\overline{d}_{1,2})
and
R_{4}(b_{1}a_{1}^{2}b_{1},\overline{d}_{1,2})
;

(v)

b_{1}=(t_{1}b_{1}a_{1}^{2}b_{1}t_{1})*b_{1}
,
[a_{1}^{2}\overline{d}_{1,2},b_{1}\overline{d}_{1,2}a_{1}^{2}b_{1}]=1
and

t_{1}b_{1}\overline{d}_{1,2}a_{1}^{2}b_{1}\overline{d}_{1,2}=a_{1}^{-2}t_{1}\overline{d}_{1,2}^{-1}b_{1}\overline{d}_{1,2}a_{1}^{2}b_{1};

(vi)

(t_{1}\overline{d}_{1,2}^{-1}b_{1}\overline{d}_{1,2}a_{1}^{2}b_{1}\overline{d}_{1,2}t_{1}^{-1})^{2}=\overline{d}_{1,2}^{-2}a_{1}^{-2}(t_{1}*a_{1}^{2})^{-2}
;

(vii)

b_{1}^{-1}a_{1}^{2}b_{1}\overline{d}_{1,2}^{-1}b_{1}^{-1}\overline{d}_{1,2}^{-2}a_{1}^{-2}b_{1}=\overline{d}_{1,2}^{-1}(t_{1}*a_{1}^{2})
and

\overline{d}_{1,2}^{-1}b_{1}^{-1}\overline{d}_{1,2}a_{1}^{2}b_{1}\overline{d}_{1,2}=b_{1}^{-1}\big((t_{1}^{-1}b_{1}^{-1}a_{1}^{-2}b_{1}^{-1})*\overline{d}_{1,2}\big)a_{1}^{2}b_{1};

(viii)

t_{1}\overline{d}_{1,2}^{-1}b_{1}\overline{d}_{1,2}a_{1}^{4}b_{1}\overline{d}_{1,2}t_{1}^{-1}b_{1}t_{1}a_{1}^{2}b_{1}\overline{d}_{1,2}^{-1}t_{1}^{-1}b_{1}t_{1}b_{1}a_{1}^{2}(t_{1}*a_{1}^{2})=1
;

(ix)

\overline{d}_{1,2}=(b_{1}a_{1}^{2}b_{1}t_{1}b_{1}a_{1}^{2}b_{1})*\overline{d}_{1,2}
;

(x)

\overline{d}_{-2,-1,1,2}a_{1}^{2}(t_{1}*a_{1}^{2})=1
.

Proof.

Write the presentation of ?THM?
for
g=2
, add relations
15
and
16
and remove
a_{2}^{2}=t_{1}*a_{1}^{2}
and
s_{1}=b_{1}a_{1}^{2}b_{1}
. We can eliminate all
\overline{d}_{i,j}
except for

using

and

. Then, from relations

,

,

and

only

and

by

. Relations

and

Finally, we turn to genus

. In this case, we are able to prove that the even spin mapping class group is generated by admissible Dehn twists, which was not previously known. Note that by

Our generating set will be given by the Dehn twists of ?THM?
along with

.

Lemma 6.6
.

The following relations hold in

.

z_{1}

Additional generators for

We obtain the following immediate consequence of ?THM?

Corollary 6.7
.

The even spin mapping class group
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{3})[\phi]
admits a presentation with generators

We can then compute the abelianization of the even spin mapping class group in every genus. Sierra
[
26
]
has obtained similar results for
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g}^{1})[\phi]
using GAP.

Corollary 6.8
.

The abelianization of
\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g})[\phi]
for
g\leq 3
is the following:

H_{1}\big(\operatorname{Mod}(\Sigma_{g})[\phi];\mathbb{Z}\big)=\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}\oplus\nicefrac{{\mathbb{Z}}}{{4\mathbb{Z}}}&\text{if $g=1$},\\
\mathbb{Z}\oplus\nicefrac{{\mathbb{Z}}}{{2\mathbb{Z}}}&\text{if $g=2$},\\
\nicefrac{{\mathbb{Z}}}{{4\mathbb{Z}}}&\text{if $g=3$}.\end{cases}

Proof.

We start from
g=1
. Consider the presentation of ?THM?
and add the commutator
[a^{2},b]=1
. Relation
R_{4}(a^{2},b)
becomes redundant, and
a^{2}b
has order
4
. This implies the statement.

For
g=2
, we start from the presentation of ?THM?
and add all commutators. Relation
(viii)
gives
t_{1}=(b_{1}^{6}a_{1}^{10})^{-1}
, and from
(v)
we get
a_{1}^{2}=\overline{d}_{1,2}^{-2}
. Hence, the abelianization is generated by
d:=\overline{d}_{1,2}
and
b:=b_{1}
. Relation
(vi)
yields
(b^{2}d^{-3})^{2}=1
, and all the other relations become superfluous.

For
g=3
the proof is exactly the same as that of ?THM?
. ∎
